**Loop quantum gravity** (**LQG**) is a theory of [quantum gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_gravity "Quantum gravity") that incorporates matter of the [Standard Model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Model "Standard Model") into the framework established for the intrinsic quantum gravity case. 
It is an attempt to develop a quantum theory of gravity based directly on [Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein "Albert Einstein")'s geometric formulation, [general relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_relativity "General relativity"). As a theory, LQG postulates that the structure of [space and time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spacetime "Spacetime") is composed of finite loops woven into an extremely fine fabric or network. These networks of loops are called [spin networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spin_network "Spin network"). The evolution of a spin network, or [spin foam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spin_foam "Spin foam"), has a scale on the order of a [Planck length](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck_length "Planck length"), approximately 10−35 meters, and smaller scales are meaningless. Consequently, not just matter, but space itself, prefers an atomic structure. 
The areas of research, which involve about 30 research groups worldwide,[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli2008-1) share the basic physical assumptions and the mathematical description of quantum space. Research has evolved in two directions: the more traditional canonical loop quantum gravity, and the newer covariant loop quantum gravity, called [spin foam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spin_foam "Spin foam") theory. The most well-developed theory that has been advanced as a direct result of loop quantum gravity is called [loop quantum cosmology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_cosmology "Loop quantum cosmology") (LQC). LQC advances the study of the early universe, incorporating the concept of the [Big Bang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Bang "Big Bang") into the broader theory of the [Big Bounce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Bounce "Big Bounce"), which envisions the Big Bang as the beginning of a [period of expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metric_expansion_of_space "Metric expansion of space"), that follows a period of contraction, which has been described as the [Big Crunch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Crunch "Big Crunch"). 
## History
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=1 "Edit section: History")]
Main article: [History of loop quantum gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_loop_quantum_gravity "History of loop quantum gravity")
In 1986, [Abhay Ashtekar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abhay_Ashtekar "Abhay Ashtekar") reformulated Einstein's general relativity in a language closer to that of the rest of fundamental physics, specifically [Yang–Mills theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yang%E2%80%93Mills_theory "Yang–Mills theory").[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-2) Shortly after, [Ted Jacobson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Jacobson "Ted Jacobson") and [Lee Smolin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Smolin "Lee Smolin") realized that the formal equation of quantum gravity, called the [Wheeler–DeWitt equation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheeler%E2%80%93DeWitt_equation "Wheeler–DeWitt equation"), admitted solutions labelled by loops when rewritten in the new [Ashtekar variables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashtekar_variables "Ashtekar variables"). [Carlo Rovelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlo_Rovelli "Carlo Rovelli") and Smolin defined a [nonperturbative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonperturbative "Nonperturbative") and background-independent quantum theory of gravity in terms of these loop solutions. [Jorge Pullin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Pullin "Jorge Pullin") and [Jerzy Lewandowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerzy_Lewandowski "Jerzy Lewandowski") understood that the intersections of the loops are essential for the consistency of the theory, and the theory should be formulated in terms of intersecting loops, or [graphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graph_\(discrete_mathematics\) "Graph \(discrete mathematics\)"). 
In 1994, Rovelli and Smolin showed that the quantum [operators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operator_\(physics\) "Operator \(physics\)") of the theory associated to area and volume have a discrete spectrum.[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-3) That is, geometry is quantized. This result defines an explicit basis of states of quantum geometry, which turned out to be labelled by [Roger Penrose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Penrose "Roger Penrose")'s [spin networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spin_network "Spin network"), which are [graphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graph_\(discrete_mathematics\) "Graph \(discrete mathematics\)") labelled by [spins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spin_\(physics\) "Spin \(physics\)"). 
The canonical version of the dynamics was established by Thomas Thiemann, who defined an anomaly-free [Hamiltonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamiltonian_\(quantum_mechanics\) "Hamiltonian \(quantum mechanics\)") operator and showed the existence of a mathematically consistent background-independent theory. The covariant, or "spin foam", version of the dynamics was developed jointly over several decades by research groups in France, Canada, UK, Poland, and Germany. It was completed in 2008, leading to the definition of a family of transition amplitudes, which in the [classical limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_limit "Classical limit") can be shown to be related to a family of truncations of general relativity.[[4]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli2011-4) The finiteness of these amplitudes was proven in 2011.[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMuxin2011064010-5)[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFairbairnMeusburger2011-6) It requires the existence of a positive [cosmological constant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmological_constant "Cosmological constant"), which is consistent with observed [acceleration in the expansion of the Universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accelerating_universe "Accelerating universe"). 
## Background independence
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=2 "Edit section: Background independence")]
LQG is formally [background independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Background_independence "Background independence"), meaning the equations of LQG are not embedded in, or dependent on, space and time (except for its invariant topology). Instead, they are expected to give rise to space and time at distances which are 10 times the [Planck length](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck_length "Planck length"). The issue of background independence in LQG still has some unresolved subtleties. For example, some derivations require a fixed choice of the [topology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topology "Topology"), while any consistent quantum theory of gravity should include topology change as a dynamical process.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]
Spacetime as a "container" over which physics takes place has no objective physical meaning and instead the gravitational interaction is represented as just one of the fields forming the world. This is known as the relationalist interpretation of spacetime. In LQG this aspect of [general relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_relativity "General relativity") is taken seriously and this symmetry is preserved by requiring that the physical states remain invariant under the generators of [diffeomorphisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffeomorphism "Diffeomorphism"). The interpretation of this condition is well understood for purely spatial [diffeomorphisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffeomorphism "Diffeomorphism"). However, the understanding of diffeomorphisms involving time (the [Hamiltonian constraint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamiltonian_constraint "Hamiltonian constraint")) is more subtle because it is related to [dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relativistic_dynamics "Relativistic dynamics") and the so-called "[problem of time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Problem_of_time "Problem of time")" in general relativity.[[7]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKauffmanSmolin1997-7) A generally accepted calculational framework to account for this constraint has yet to be found.[[8]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmolin2006196''ff''-8)[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli200413ff-9) A plausible candidate for the quantum Hamiltonian constraint is the operator introduced by Thiemann.[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThiemann1996257%E2%80%93264-10)
## Constraints and their Poisson bracket algebra
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section: Constraints and their Poisson bracket algebra")]
Main articles: [Poisson bracket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poisson_bracket "Poisson bracket") and [Hamiltonian constraint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamiltonian_constraint "Hamiltonian constraint")
### Dirac observables
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section: Dirac observables")]
The constraints define a constraint surface in the original phase space. The [gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauge_theory "Gauge theory") motions of the constraints apply to all phase space but have the feature that they leave the constraint surface where it is, and thus the orbit of a point in the hypersurface under [gauge transformations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauge_transformations "Gauge transformations") will be an orbit entirely within it. [Dirac observables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirac_observables "Dirac observables") are defined as [phase space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phase_space "Phase space") functions,  O {\displaystyle O} ![{\\displaystyle O}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9d70e1d0d87e2ef1092ea1ffe2923d9933ff18fc), that [Poisson commute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poisson_commutativity "Poisson commutativity") with all the constraints when the constraint equations are imposed,  { G j , O } G j = C a = H = 0 = { C a , O } G j = C a = H = 0 = { H , O } G j = C a = H = 0 = 0 , {\displaystyle \\{G_{j},O\\}_{G_{j}=C_{a}=H=0}=\\{C_{a},O\\}_{G_{j}=C_{a}=H=0}=\\{H,O\\}_{G_{j}=C_{a}=H=0}=0,} ![{\\displaystyle \\{G_{j},O\\}_{G_{j}=C_{a}=H=0}=\\{C_{a},O\\}_{G_{j}=C_{a}=H=0}=\\{H,O\\}_{G_{j}=C_{a}=H=0}=0,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4e6cb0c0d3f1652abe3c7cf326fa4103aca08267) that is, they are quantities defined on the constraint surface that are invariant under the gauge transformations of the theory. 
Then, solving only the constraint  G j = 0 {\displaystyle G_{j}=0} ![{\\displaystyle G_{j}=0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9d98f46af0862578e8fbc5c2c8a68b3cbb72531b) and determining the Dirac observables with respect to it leads us back to the [Arnowitt–Deser–Misner (ADM) phase space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ADM_formalism "ADM formalism") with constraints  H , C a {\displaystyle H,C_{a}} ![{\\displaystyle H,C_{a}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/31a88db699bf0f6a8332904ace2a4c14e66c5019). The dynamics of general relativity is generated by the constraints, it can be shown that six Einstein equations describing time evolution (really a gauge transformation) can be obtained by calculating the Poisson brackets of the three-metric and its conjugate momentum with a linear combination of the spatial diffeomorphism and Hamiltonian constraint. The vanishing of the constraints, giving the physical phase space, are the four other Einstein equations.[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaezde_Muniain1994Part_III,_chapter_4-11)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=5 "Edit section: Quantization of the constraints – the equations of quantum general relativity")]
### Pre-history and Ashtekar new variables
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section: Pre-history and Ashtekar new variables")]
Main articles: [Frame fields in general relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frame_fields_in_general_relativity "Frame fields in general relativity"), [Ashtekar variables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashtekar_variables "Ashtekar variables"), and [Self-dual Palatini action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-dual_Palatini_action "Self-dual Palatini action")
Many of the technical problems in canonical quantum gravity revolve around the constraints. Canonical general relativity was originally formulated in terms of metric variables, but there seemed to be insurmountable mathematical difficulties in promoting the constraints to [quantum operators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_operator "Quantum operator") because of their highly non-linear dependence on the canonical variables. The equations were much simplified with the introduction of Ashtekar's new variables. Ashtekar variables describe canonical general relativity in terms of a new pair of canonical variables closer to those of gauge theories. The first step consists of using densitized triads  E ~ i a {\displaystyle {\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/767dbb0905f00395d6500133094c018177511f0f) (a triad  E i a {\displaystyle E_{i}^{a}} ![{\\displaystyle E_{i}^{a}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/aee38c50460ff5641529b76e9ff0f5135da8869d) is simply three orthogonal vector fields labeled by  i = 1 , 2 , 3 {\displaystyle i=1,2,3} ![{\\displaystyle i=1,2,3}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4a87b7e5d81c62b040a35078417fb9294b1ec7c7) and the densitized triad is defined by  E ~ i a = det ( q ) E i a {\textstyle {\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}={\sqrt {\det(q)}}E_{i}^{a}} ![{\\textstyle {\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}={\\sqrt {\\det\(q\)}}E_{i}^{a}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f8eaefa54172e5a1dbff0d235b01b272d2e817d5)) to encode information about the spatial metric,  det ( q ) q a b = E ~ i a E ~ j b δ i j . {\displaystyle \det(q)q^{ab}={\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}{\tilde {E}}_{j}^{b}\delta ^{ij}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\det\(q\)q^{ab}={\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}{\\tilde {E}}_{j}^{b}\\delta ^{ij}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4a1f3568aab5191ed68b422a6dc86e7137fc2dba) (where  δ i j {\displaystyle \delta ^{ij}} ![{\\displaystyle \\delta ^{ij}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/742f3fcc9c517aab2ce39e9ff3a4e07e696ce381) is the flat space metric, and the above equation expresses that  q a b {\displaystyle q^{ab}} ![{\\displaystyle q^{ab}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c358708655dd2ab29d13ea8be51074e00d39a69a), when written in terms of the basis  E i a {\displaystyle E_{i}^{a}} ![{\\displaystyle E_{i}^{a}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/aee38c50460ff5641529b76e9ff0f5135da8869d), is locally flat). (Formulating general relativity with triads instead of metrics was not new.) The densitized triads are not unique, and in fact one can perform a local in space [rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotation "Rotation") with respect to the internal indices  i {\displaystyle i} ![{\\displaystyle i}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/add78d8608ad86e54951b8c8bd6c8d8416533d20). The canonically conjugate variable is related to the extrinsic curvature by  K a i = K a b E ~ a i / det ( q ) {\textstyle K_{a}^{i}=K_{ab}{\tilde {E}}^{ai}/{\sqrt {\det(q)}}} ![{\\textstyle K_{a}^{i}=K_{ab}{\\tilde {E}}^{ai}/{\\sqrt {\\det\(q\)}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7977cdcf69de0c062ae0f33dc86afcbe741e0e0b). But problems similar to using the metric formulation arise when one tries to quantize the theory. Ashtekar's new insight was to introduce a new configuration variable,  A a i = Γ a i − i K a i {\displaystyle A_{a}^{i}=\Gamma _{a}^{i}-iK_{a}^{i}} ![{\\displaystyle A_{a}^{i}=\\Gamma _{a}^{i}-iK_{a}^{i}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/be304706bbf7bd1ac2d9a3cc0f497a132b1c8372) that behaves as a complex  SU ⁡ ( 2 ) {\displaystyle \operatorname {SU} (2)} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {SU} \(2\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/411cfb70f758af1b407762245b1b07f6ee96c526) connection where  Γ a i {\displaystyle \Gamma _{a}^{i}} ![{\\displaystyle \\Gamma _{a}^{i}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ac6fe0d7daeb908bc952a8e323d4492ac42e6b49) is related to the so-called [spin connection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spin_connection "Spin connection") via  Γ a i = Γ a j k ϵ j k i {\displaystyle \Gamma _{a}^{i}=\Gamma _{ajk}\epsilon ^{jki}} ![{\\displaystyle \\Gamma _{a}^{i}=\\Gamma _{ajk}\\epsilon ^{jki}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/66ee5b7fd6a2ef12d0cfd879b9d957b852644198). Here  A a i {\displaystyle A_{a}^{i}} ![{\\displaystyle A_{a}^{i}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3da43ceb9301c57ce8f50a0aac65913e69ec18d1) is called the chiral spin connection. It defines a covariant derivative  D a {\displaystyle {\mathcal {D}}_{a}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\mathcal {D}}_{a}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2b5e722f7951356b77173c1ce2c9fb1b967ed4c5). It turns out that  E ~ i a {\displaystyle {\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/767dbb0905f00395d6500133094c018177511f0f) is the conjugate momentum of  A a i {\displaystyle A_{a}^{i}} ![{\\displaystyle A_{a}^{i}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3da43ceb9301c57ce8f50a0aac65913e69ec18d1), and together these form Ashtekar's new variables. 
The expressions for the constraints in Ashtekar variables; Gauss's theorem, the spatial diffeomorphism constraint and the (densitized) Hamiltonian constraint then read:  G i = D a E ~ i a = 0 {\displaystyle G^{i}={\mathcal {D}}_{a}{\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}=0} ![{\\displaystyle G^{i}={\\mathcal {D}}_{a}{\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}=0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7f4351baf61c14fa366a67adf2023e24e191e990) C a = E ~ i b F a b i − A a i ( D b E ~ i b ) = V a − A a i G i = 0 , {\displaystyle C_{a}={\tilde {E}}_{i}^{b}F_{ab}^{i}-A_{a}^{i}({\mathcal {D}}_{b}{\tilde {E}}_{i}^{b})=V_{a}-A_{a}^{i}G^{i}=0,} ![{\\displaystyle C_{a}={\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{b}F_{ab}^{i}-A_{a}^{i}\({\\mathcal {D}}_{b}{\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{b}\)=V_{a}-A_{a}^{i}G^{i}=0,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/73b89f3ae1eccbb6ee45317b1922b2db47f09024) H ~ = ϵ i j k E ~ i a E ~ j b F a b k = 0 {\displaystyle {\tilde {H}}=\epsilon _{ijk}{\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}{\tilde {E}}_{j}^{b}F_{ab}^{k}=0} ![{\\displaystyle {\\tilde {H}}=\\epsilon _{ijk}{\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}{\\tilde {E}}_{j}^{b}F_{ab}^{k}=0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/0f035ec4cba7b0bb5faa8cf3c3886a7522b53be4) respectively, where  F a b i {\displaystyle F_{ab}^{i}} ![{\\displaystyle F_{ab}^{i}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/fe73065cedbcc1ff253979009cce75b12ce162b3) is the field strength tensor of the connection  A a i {\displaystyle A_{a}^{i}} ![{\\displaystyle A_{a}^{i}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3da43ceb9301c57ce8f50a0aac65913e69ec18d1) and where  V a {\displaystyle V_{a}} ![{\\displaystyle V_{a}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b26205fe6c67ed47c72959a921fe7fd9c1c250ed) is referred to as the vector constraint. The above-mentioned local in space rotational invariance is the original of the  SU ⁡ ( 2 ) {\displaystyle \operatorname {SU} (2)} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {SU} \(2\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/411cfb70f758af1b407762245b1b07f6ee96c526) gauge invariance here expressed by Gauss's theorem. Note that these constraints are polynomial in the fundamental variables, unlike the constraints in the metric formulation. This dramatic simplification seemed to open up the way to quantizing the constraints. (See the article [Self-dual Palatini action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-dual_Palatini_action "Self-dual Palatini action") for a derivation of Ashtekar's formalism). 
With Ashtekar's new variables, given the configuration variable  A a i {\displaystyle A_{a}^{i}} ![{\\displaystyle A_{a}^{i}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3da43ceb9301c57ce8f50a0aac65913e69ec18d1), it is natural to consider wavefunctions  Ψ ( A a i ) {\displaystyle \Psi (A_{a}^{i})} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \(A_{a}^{i}\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/fe5364f1c8a04fec14583e81a658740c5ddb1d6d). This is the connection representation. It is analogous to ordinary quantum mechanics with configuration variable  q {\displaystyle q} ![{\\displaystyle q}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/06809d64fa7c817ffc7e323f85997f783dbdf71d) and wavefunctions  ψ ( q ) {\displaystyle \psi (q)} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(q\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/672f8a61ddc5fedd13f15ca31cd2ab8c044098a4). The configuration variable gets promoted to a quantum operator via:  A ^ a i Ψ ( A ) = A a i Ψ ( A ) , {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}_{a}^{i}\Psi (A)=A_{a}^{i}\Psi (A),} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}_{a}^{i}\\Psi \(A\)=A_{a}^{i}\\Psi \(A\),}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b0265c5eb640c946a09736487e14f97ef0c4ea22) (analogous to  q ^ ψ ( q ) = q ψ ( q ) {\displaystyle {\hat {q}}\psi (q)=q\psi (q)} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {q}}\\psi \(q\)=q\\psi \(q\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/da9f669d84fdb413ee41fda09ed446992d2053a4)) and the triads are (functional) derivatives,  E i a ~ ^ Ψ ( A ) = − i δ Ψ ( A ) δ A a i . {\displaystyle {\hat {\tilde {E_{i}^{a}}}}\Psi (A)=-i{\delta \Psi (A) \over \delta A_{a}^{i}}.} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {\\tilde {E_{i}^{a}}}}\\Psi \(A\)=-i{\\delta \\Psi \(A\) \\over \\delta A_{a}^{i}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/124b649cc8638e0ed99deebd47b3a1e4e9ead660) (analogous to  p ^ ψ ( q ) = − i ℏ d ψ ( q ) / d q {\displaystyle {\hat {p}}\psi (q)=-i\hbar d\psi (q)/dq} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {p}}\\psi \(q\)=-i\\hbar d\\psi \(q\)/dq}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/288216b5af199a0b2bfcf6929fecf8e7d3124764)). In passing over to the quantum theory the constraints become operators on a kinematic Hilbert space (the unconstrained  SU ⁡ ( 2 ) {\displaystyle \operatorname {SU} (2)} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {SU} \(2\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/411cfb70f758af1b407762245b1b07f6ee96c526) Yang–Mills Hilbert space). Note that different ordering of the  A {\displaystyle A} ![{\\displaystyle A}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7daff47fa58cdfd29dc333def748ff5fa4c923e3)'s and  E ~ {\displaystyle {\tilde {E}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\tilde {E}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/18f629216bbaa99cf55e7cbf0f27fda050c95cc6)'s when replacing the  E ~ {\displaystyle {\tilde {E}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\tilde {E}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/18f629216bbaa99cf55e7cbf0f27fda050c95cc6)'s with derivatives give rise to different operators – the choice made is called the factor ordering and should be chosen via physical reasoning. Formally they read  G ^ j | ψ ⟩ = 0 {\displaystyle {\hat {G}}_{j}\vert \psi \rangle =0} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {G}}_{j}\\vert \\psi \\rangle =0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d378d4903e1da2b0dcfaddcdd6034df7b27a1174) C ^ a | ψ ⟩ = 0 {\displaystyle {\hat {C}}_{a}\vert \psi \rangle =0} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {C}}_{a}\\vert \\psi \\rangle =0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c315cf25e18a757597765e94505b45d940ea2158) H ~ ^ | ψ ⟩ = 0. {\displaystyle {\hat {\tilde {H}}}\vert \psi \rangle =0.} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {\\tilde {H}}}\\vert \\psi \\rangle =0.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2358ab82e92740296ce1ef220c3fccff363f130e)
There are still problems in properly defining all these equations and solving them. For example, the Hamiltonian constraint Ashtekar worked with was the densitized version instead of the original Hamiltonian, that is, he worked with  H ~ = det ( q ) H {\textstyle {\tilde {H}}={\sqrt {\det(q)}}H} ![{\\textstyle {\\tilde {H}}={\\sqrt {\\det\(q\)}}H}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/1d6ad1f3d6ef6ab84735c085f32abda2c201ea70). There were serious difficulties in promoting this quantity to a quantum operator. Moreover, although Ashtekar variables had the virtue of simplifying the Hamiltonian, they are complex. When one quantizes the theory, it is difficult to ensure that one recovers real general relativity as opposed to complex general relativity. 
### Quantum constraints as the equations of quantum general relativity
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=7 "Edit section: Quantum constraints as the equations of quantum general relativity")]
The classical result of the Poisson bracket of the smeared Gauss' law  G ( λ ) = ∫ d 3 x λ j ( D a E a ) j {\textstyle G(\lambda )=\int d^{3}x\lambda ^{j}(D_{a}E^{a})^{j}} ![{\\textstyle G\(\\lambda \)=\\int d^{3}x\\lambda ^{j}\(D_{a}E^{a}\)^{j}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9325acf1f812360a00aacbfee9c5ceab083f2a7b) with the connections is  { G ( λ ) , A a i } = ∂ a λ i + g ϵ i j k A a j λ k = ( D a λ ) i . {\displaystyle \\{G(\lambda ),A_{a}^{i}\\}=\partial _{a}\lambda ^{i}+g\epsilon ^{ijk}A_{a}^{j}\lambda ^{k}=(D_{a}\lambda )^{i}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\{G\(\\lambda \),A_{a}^{i}\\}=\\partial _{a}\\lambda ^{i}+g\\epsilon ^{ijk}A_{a}^{j}\\lambda ^{k}=\(D_{a}\\lambda \)^{i}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/30dc5b442ebbfb6cfd4d7357eab99999d4709fea)
The quantum Gauss' law reads  G ^ j Ψ ( A ) = − i D a δ λ Ψ [ A ] δ A a j = 0. {\displaystyle {\hat {G}}_{j}\Psi (A)=-iD_{a}{\delta \lambda \Psi [A] \over \delta A_{a}^{j}}=0.} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {G}}_{j}\\Psi \(A\)=-iD_{a}{\\delta \\lambda \\Psi \[A\] \\over \\delta A_{a}^{j}}=0.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/60ce26223748ffe84f2065569958e77d7dfb1c17)
If one smears the quantum Gauss' law and study its action on the quantum state one finds that the action of the constraint on the quantum state is equivalent to shifting the argument of  Ψ {\displaystyle \Psi } ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f5471531a3fe80741a839bc98d49fae862a6439a) by an infinitesimal (in the sense of the parameter  λ {\displaystyle \lambda } ![{\\displaystyle \\lambda }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b43d0ea3c9c025af1be9128e62a18fa74bedda2a) small) gauge transformation,  [ 1 + ∫ d 3 x λ j ( x ) G ^ j ] Ψ ( A ) = Ψ [ A + D λ ] = Ψ [ A ] , {\displaystyle \left[1+\int d^{3}x\lambda ^{j}(x){\hat {G}}_{j}\right]\Psi (A)=\Psi [A+D\lambda ]=\Psi [A],} ![{\\displaystyle \\left\[1+\\int d^{3}x\\lambda ^{j}\(x\){\\hat {G}}_{j}\\right\]\\Psi \(A\)=\\Psi \[A+D\\lambda \]=\\Psi \[A\],}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/08fa4f29b3c28c30e2be1b0664f40bb73bb27b2c) and the last identity comes from the fact that the constraint annihilates the state. So the constraint, as a quantum operator, is imposing the same symmetry that its vanishing imposed classically: it is telling us that the functions  Ψ [ A ] {\displaystyle \Psi [A]} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \[A\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/67ff914baeb5bd928ed9ccd89572a317f07999d3) have to be gauge invariant functions of the connection. The same idea is true for the other constraints. 
Therefore, the two step process in the classical theory of solving the constraints  C I = 0 {\displaystyle C_{I}=0} ![{\\displaystyle C_{I}=0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ecd8458874ed69dbb7ecd1155f4628e700ebce17) (equivalent to solving the admissibility conditions for the initial data) and looking for the gauge orbits (solving the 'evolution' equations) is replaced by a one step process in the quantum theory, namely looking for solutions  Ψ {\displaystyle \Psi } ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f5471531a3fe80741a839bc98d49fae862a6439a) of the quantum equations  C ^ I Ψ = 0 {\displaystyle {\hat {C}}_{I}\Psi =0} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {C}}_{I}\\Psi =0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ec9c1731fbefcde0e98c636d28cdedd68a72cbe7). This is because it solves the constraint at the quantum level and it simultaneously looks for states that are gauge invariant because  C ^ I {\displaystyle {\hat {C}}_{I}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {C}}_{I}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2e8f88573292f01d1858b27f5cd8a14c69f42c3a) is the quantum generator of gauge transformations (gauge invariant functions are constant along the gauge orbits and thus characterize them).[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThiemann200341%E2%80%93135-12) Recall that, at the classical level, solving the admissibility conditions and evolution equations was equivalent to solving all of Einstein's field equations, this underlines the central role of the quantum constraint equations in canonical quantum gravity. 
### Introduction of the loop representation
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section: Introduction of the loop representation")]
Main articles: [Holonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holonomy "Holonomy"), [Wilson loop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilson_loop "Wilson loop"), and [Knot invariant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knot_invariant "Knot invariant")
It was in particular the inability to have good control over the space of solutions to Gauss's law and spatial diffeomorphism constraints that led Rovelli and Smolin to consider the [loop representation in gauge theories and quantum gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_representation_in_gauge_theories_and_quantum_gravity "Loop representation in gauge theories and quantum gravity").[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelliSmolin19881155%E2%80%931958-13)
LQG includes the concept of a [holonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holonomy "Holonomy"). A holonomy is a measure of how much the initial and final values of a spinor or vector differ after [parallel transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_transport "Parallel transport") around a closed loop; it is denoted  h γ [ A ] . {\displaystyle h_{\gamma }[A].} ![{\\displaystyle h_{\\gamma }\[A\].}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/dcf41d5d8ed320a233d7a04c8353ed56a8efe6b2)
Knowledge of the holonomies is equivalent to knowledge of the connection, up to gauge equivalence. Holonomies can also be associated with an edge; under a Gauss Law these transform as  ( h e ′ ) α β = U α γ − 1 ( x ) ( h e ) γ σ U σ β ( y ) . {\displaystyle (h'_{e})_{\alpha \beta }=U_{\alpha \gamma }^{-1}(x)(h_{e})_{\gamma \sigma }U_{\sigma \beta }(y).} ![{\\displaystyle \(h'_{e}\)_{\\alpha \\beta }=U_{\\alpha \\gamma }^{-1}\(x\)\(h_{e}\)_{\\gamma \\sigma }U_{\\sigma \\beta }\(y\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c41c8d28bc50b02abceb08b7324a43cc83c73b2e)
For a closed loop  x = y {\displaystyle x=y} ![{\\displaystyle x=y}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/409a91214d63eabe46ec10ff3cbba689ab687366) and assuming  α = β {\displaystyle \alpha =\beta } ![{\\displaystyle \\alpha =\\beta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ef6894a6c2f414b03c984a1c7f0639063b0020ab), yields  ( h e ′ ) α α = U α γ − 1 ( x ) ( h e ) γ σ U σ α ( x ) = [ U σ α ( x ) U α γ − 1 ( x ) ] ( h e ) γ σ = δ σ γ ( h e ) γ σ = ( h e ) γ γ {\displaystyle (h'_{e})_{\alpha \alpha }=U_{\alpha \gamma }^{-1}(x)(h_{e})_{\gamma \sigma }U_{\sigma \alpha }(x)=[U_{\sigma \alpha }(x)U_{\alpha \gamma }^{-1}(x)](h_{e})_{\gamma \sigma }=\delta _{\sigma \gamma }(h_{e})_{\gamma \sigma }=(h_{e})_{\gamma \gamma }} ![{\\displaystyle \(h'_{e}\)_{\\alpha \\alpha }=U_{\\alpha \\gamma }^{-1}\(x\)\(h_{e}\)_{\\gamma \\sigma }U_{\\sigma \\alpha }\(x\)=\[U_{\\sigma \\alpha }\(x\)U_{\\alpha \\gamma }^{-1}\(x\)\]\(h_{e}\)_{\\gamma \\sigma }=\\delta _{\\sigma \\gamma }\(h_{e}\)_{\\gamma \\sigma }=\(h_{e}\)_{\\gamma \\gamma }}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/33f90738e5a7f7ab8a65b5628ff81387ab6c33ed) or  Tr ⁡ h γ ′ = Tr ⁡ h γ . {\displaystyle \operatorname {Tr} h'_{\gamma }=\operatorname {Tr} h_{\gamma }.} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {Tr} h'_{\\gamma }=\\operatorname {Tr} h_{\\gamma }.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/5fb40fa68a672c55f82a3d1ee3b0fee218a4b3e5)
The trace of an holonomy around a closed loop is written  W γ [ A ] {\displaystyle W_{\gamma }[A]} ![{\\displaystyle W_{\\gamma }\[A\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9c819a5d3e9f069d989a2ad345c11829e02f1c75) and is called a Wilson loop. Thus Wilson loops are gauge invariant. The explicit form of the Holonomy is  h γ [ A ] = P exp ⁡ { − ∫ γ 0 γ 1 d s γ ˙ a A a i ( γ ( s ) ) T i } {\displaystyle h_{\gamma }[A]={\mathcal {P}}\exp \left\\{-\int _{\gamma _{0}}^{\gamma _{1}}ds{\dot {\gamma }}^{a}A_{a}^{i}(\gamma (s))T_{i}\right\\}} ![{\\displaystyle h_{\\gamma }\[A\]={\\mathcal {P}}\\exp \\left\\{-\\int _{\\gamma _{0}}^{\\gamma _{1}}ds{\\dot {\\gamma }}^{a}A_{a}^{i}\(\\gamma \(s\)\)T_{i}\\right\\}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ee943f18274e25b9122f9c2193b12fd94f20fe4f) where  γ {\displaystyle \gamma } ![{\\displaystyle \\gamma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a223c880b0ce3da8f64ee33c4f0010beee400b1a) is the curve along which the holonomy is evaluated, and  s {\displaystyle s} ![{\\displaystyle s}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/01d131dfd7673938b947072a13a9744fe997e632) is a parameter along the curve,  P {\displaystyle {\mathcal {P}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\mathcal {P}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/10d6ec962de5797ba4f161c40e66dca74ae95cc6) denotes path ordering meaning factors for smaller values of  s {\displaystyle s} ![{\\displaystyle s}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/01d131dfd7673938b947072a13a9744fe997e632) appear to the left, and  T i {\displaystyle T_{i}} ![{\\displaystyle T_{i}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a8dd1c50cb9436474f83624c3f679ccf3eebbfef) are matrices that satisfy the  SU ⁡ ( 2 ) {\displaystyle \operatorname {SU} (2)} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {SU} \(2\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/411cfb70f758af1b407762245b1b07f6ee96c526) algebra  [ T i , T j ] = 2 i ϵ i j k T k . {\displaystyle [T^{i},T^{j}]=2i\epsilon ^{ijk}T_{k}.} ![{\\displaystyle \[T^{i},T^{j}\]=2i\\epsilon ^{ijk}T_{k}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/99a93350ce57bfa6e6acc3b5a64b21f8d19e2a16)
The [Pauli matrices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauli_matrices "Pauli matrices") satisfy the above relation. It turns out that there are infinitely many more examples of sets of matrices that satisfy these relations, where each set comprises  ( N + 1 ) × ( N + 1 ) {\displaystyle (N+1)\times (N+1)} ![{\\displaystyle \(N+1\)\\times \(N+1\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/0a0a9d85bd83e21f9315e17ff004ee4471fd4179) matrices with  N = 1 , 2 , 3 , … {\displaystyle N=1,2,3,\dots } ![{\\displaystyle N=1,2,3,\\dots }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e043bd2cd641100ce411cc503d8a037a5db63f2d), and where none of these can be thought to 'decompose' into two or more examples of lower dimension. They are called different [irreducible representations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreducible_representations "Irreducible representations") of the  SU ⁡ ( 2 ) {\displaystyle \operatorname {SU} (2)} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {SU} \(2\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/411cfb70f758af1b407762245b1b07f6ee96c526) algebra. The most fundamental representation being the Pauli matrices. The holonomy is labelled by a half integer  N / 2 {\displaystyle N/2} ![{\\displaystyle N/2}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/45c51c21b2bc7ea5e2fcae8f0f4aa49f6f19ebaf) according to the irreducible representation used. 
The use of [Wilson loops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilson_loop "Wilson loop") explicitly solves the Gauss gauge constraint. [Loop representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_representation "Loop representation") is required to handle the spatial diffeomorphism constraint. With Wilson loops as a basis, any Gauss gauge invariant function expands as,  Ψ [ A ] = ∑ γ Ψ [ γ ] W γ [ A ] . {\displaystyle \Psi [A]=\sum _{\gamma }\Psi [\gamma ]W_{\gamma }[A].} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \[A\]=\\sum _{\\gamma }\\Psi \[\\gamma \]W_{\\gamma }\[A\].}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a66d98bf3ede69de95f83e797068a4b4d61c7e35)
This is called the loop transform and is analogous to the momentum representation in quantum mechanics (see [Position and momentum space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Position_and_momentum_space "Position and momentum space")). The QM representation has a basis of states  exp ⁡ ( i k x ) {\displaystyle \exp(ikx)} ![{\\displaystyle \\exp\(ikx\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d702d25204240b139c4e68e10251b0be5d887f59) labelled by a number  k {\displaystyle k} ![{\\displaystyle k}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c3c9a2c7b599b37105512c5d570edc034056dd40) and expands as  ψ [ x ] = ∫ d k ψ ( k ) exp ⁡ ( i k x ) . {\displaystyle \psi [x]=\int dk\psi (k)\exp(ikx).} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \[x\]=\\int dk\\psi \(k\)\\exp\(ikx\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d85a46131c1ad49af54e081e983a0ed8d63f9e59) and works with the coefficients of the expansion  ψ ( k ) . {\displaystyle \psi (k).} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(k\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ee5fe1e12a76899d6c598ef0c5b4a25d5b2ef7ab)
The inverse loop transform is defined by  Ψ [ γ ] = ∫ [ d A ] Ψ [ A ] W γ [ A ] . {\displaystyle \Psi [\gamma ]=\int [dA]\Psi [A]W_{\gamma }[A].} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \[\\gamma \]=\\int \[dA\]\\Psi \[A\]W_{\\gamma }\[A\].}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cc2e9d9ed1967f1603f6e0846288389f32024bc6)
This defines the loop representation. Given an operator  O ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {O}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {O}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8d084213a448be2cfb9134294127a7243386773a) in the connection representation,  Φ [ A ] = O ^ Ψ [ A ] E q 1 , {\displaystyle \Phi [A]={\hat {O}}\Psi [A]\qquad Eq\;1,} ![{\\displaystyle \\Phi \[A\]={\\hat {O}}\\Psi \[A\]\\qquad Eq\\;1,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/27457469b09a6bb73bf1d6e8bc5aff981faca49a) one should define the corresponding operator  O ^ ′ {\displaystyle {\hat {O}}'} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {O}}'}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/97f615e0f1769616057492bbce37c92122ac44b9) on  Ψ [ γ ] {\displaystyle \Psi [\gamma ]} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \[\\gamma \]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6f8d7cdd9ae00dddc142ba1e03d35be57173be57) in the loop representation via,  Φ [ γ ] = O ^ ′ Ψ [ γ ] E q 2 , {\displaystyle \Phi [\gamma ]={\hat {O}}'\Psi [\gamma ]\qquad Eq\;2,} ![{\\displaystyle \\Phi \[\\gamma \]={\\hat {O}}'\\Psi \[\\gamma \]\\qquad Eq\\;2,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/eeffe74d223b1d8cb5ef940e1ebd1c23d98a5317) where  Φ [ γ ] {\displaystyle \Phi [\gamma ]} ![{\\displaystyle \\Phi \[\\gamma \]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/bd437ac38e2d2510ea77d74b23159f8a837c7d8c) is defined by the usual inverse loop transform,  Φ [ γ ] = ∫ [ d A ] Φ [ A ] W γ [ A ] E q 3. {\displaystyle \Phi [\gamma ]=\int [dA]\Phi [A]W_{\gamma }[A]\qquad Eq\;3.} ![{\\displaystyle \\Phi \[\\gamma \]=\\int \[dA\]\\Phi \[A\]W_{\\gamma }\[A\]\\qquad Eq\\;3.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4e096d1f0f4e4bdb50e3ad1d2938a0076853db0c)
A transformation formula giving the action of the operator  O ^ ′ {\displaystyle {\hat {O}}'} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {O}}'}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/97f615e0f1769616057492bbce37c92122ac44b9) on  Ψ [ γ ] {\displaystyle \Psi [\gamma ]} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \[\\gamma {O}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8d084213a448be2cfb9134294127a7243386773a) on  Ψ [ A ] {\displaystyle \Psi [A]} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi Eq\\;2}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6de3ad07c7adbacfd94e49612ec91c5cebce0a4c) with the R.H.S. of  E q 3 {\displaystyle Eq\;3} ![{\\displaystyle Eq\\;3}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4bfd4a4fa296fb81a234ec0bb1a4fa38d47a521c) with  E q 1 {\displaystyle Eq\;1} ![{\\displaystyle Eq\\;1}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8c25706ea5ba27f047fa2df843c6c65924c846c8) substituted into  E q 3 {\displaystyle Eq\;3} ![{\\displaystyle Eq\\;3}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4bfd4a4fa296fb81a234ec0bb1a4fa38d47a521c), namely  O ^ ′ Ψ [ γ ] = ∫ [ d A ] W γ [ A ] O ^ Ψ [ A ] , {\displaystyle {\hat {O}}'\Psi [\gamma ]=\int [dA]W_{\gamma }[A]{\hat {O}}\Psi [A],} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {O}}'\\Psi \[\\gamma \]=\\int \[dA\]W_{\\gamma }\[A\]{\\hat {O}}\\Psi \[A\],}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6a543ce03ec8af24844b0d192255b2d2796a4f3b) or  O ^ ′ Ψ [ γ ] = ∫ [ d A ] ( O ^ † W γ [ A ] ) Ψ [ A ] , {\displaystyle {\hat {O}}'\Psi [\gamma ]=\int [dA]({\hat {O}}^{\dagger }W_{\gamma }[A])\Psi [A],} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {O}}'\\Psi \[\\gamma \]=\\int \[dA\]\({\\hat {O}}^{\\dagger }W_{\\gamma }\[A\]\)\\Psi \[A\],}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c67103fe0425024a17fba51c2bef15c240bc9b1a) where  O ^ † {\displaystyle {\hat {O}}^{\dagger }} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {O}}^{\\dagger }}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/780aa29c7d84630d0e25449099a55995613b5e7f) means the operator  O ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {O}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {O}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8d084213a448be2cfb9134294127a7243386773a) but with the reverse factor ordering (remember from simple quantum mechanics where the product of operators is reversed under conjugation). The action of this operator on the Wilson loop is evaluated as a calculation in the connection representation and the result is rearranged purely as a manipulation in terms of loops (with regard to the action on the Wilson loop, the chosen transformed operator is the one with the opposite factor ordering compared to the one used for its action on wavefunctions  Ψ [ A ] {\displaystyle \Psi [A]} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \[A\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/67ff914baeb5bd928ed9ccd89572a317f07999d3)). This gives the physical meaning of the operator  O ^ ′ {\displaystyle {\hat {O}}'} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {O}}'}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/97f615e0f1769616057492bbce37c92122ac44b9). For example, if  O ^ † {\displaystyle {\hat {O}}^{\dagger }} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {O}}^{\\dagger }}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/780aa29c7d84630d0e25449099a55995613b5e7f) corresponded to a spatial diffeomorphism, then this can be thought of as keeping the connection field  A {\displaystyle A} ![{\\displaystyle A}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7daff47fa58cdfd29dc333def748ff5fa4c923e3) of  W γ [ A ] {\displaystyle W_{\gamma }[A]} ![{\\displaystyle W_{\\gamma }\[A\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9c819a5d3e9f069d989a2ad345c11829e02f1c75) where it is while performing a spatial diffeomorphism on  γ {\displaystyle \gamma } ![{\\displaystyle \\gamma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a223c880b0ce3da8f64ee33c4f0010beee400b1a) instead. Therefore, the meaning of  O ^ ′ {\displaystyle {\hat {O}}'} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {O}}'}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/97f615e0f1769616057492bbce37c92122ac44b9) is a spatial diffeomorphism on  γ {\displaystyle \gamma } ![{\\displaystyle \\gamma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a223c880b0ce3da8f64ee33c4f0010beee400b1a), the argument of  Ψ [ γ ] {\displaystyle \Psi [\gamma ]} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \[\\gamma \]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6f8d7cdd9ae00dddc142ba1e03d35be57173be57). 
In the loop representation, the spatial diffeomorphism constraint is solved by considering functions of loops  Ψ [ γ ] {\displaystyle \Psi [\gamma ]} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \[\\gamma \]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6f8d7cdd9ae00dddc142ba1e03d35be57173be57) that are invariant under spatial diffeomorphisms of the loop  γ {\displaystyle \gamma } ![{\\displaystyle \\gamma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a223c880b0ce3da8f64ee33c4f0010beee400b1a). That is, [knot invariants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knot_invariant "Knot invariant") are used. This opens up an unexpected connection between [knot theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knot_theory "Knot theory") and quantum gravity. 
Any collection of non-intersecting Wilson loops satisfy Ashtekar's quantum Hamiltonian constraint. Using a particular ordering of terms and replacing  E ~ i a {\displaystyle {\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/767dbb0905f00395d6500133094c018177511f0f) by a derivative, the action of the quantum Hamiltonian constraint on a Wilson loop is  H ~ ^ † W γ [ A ] = − ϵ i j k F ^ a b k δ δ A a i δ δ A b j W γ [ A ] . {\displaystyle {\hat {\tilde {H}}}^{\dagger }W_{\gamma }[A]=-\epsilon _{ijk}{\hat {F}}_{ab}^{k}{\frac {\delta }{\delta A_{a}^{i}}}{\frac {\delta }{\delta A_{b}^{j}}}W_{\gamma }[A].} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {\\tilde {H}}}^{\\dagger }W_{\\gamma }\[A\]=-\\epsilon _{ijk}{\\hat {F}}_{ab}^{k}{\\frac {\\delta }{\\delta A_{a}^{i}}}{\\frac {\\delta }{\\delta A_{b}^{j}}}W_{\\gamma }\[A\].}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7ba6670026a2fc0a21e03a90943a09331a357ff4)
When a derivative is taken it brings down the tangent vector,  γ ˙ a {\displaystyle {\dot {\gamma }}^{a}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\dot {\\gamma }}^{a}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ab63ff919cd5021367eefd0d38b54b054b8dabdb), of the loop,  γ {\displaystyle \gamma } ![{\\displaystyle \\gamma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a223c880b0ce3da8f64ee33c4f0010beee400b1a). So,  F ^ a b i γ ˙ a γ ˙ b . {\displaystyle {\hat {F}}_{ab}^{i}{\dot {\gamma }}^{a}{\dot {\gamma }}^{b}.} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {F}}_{ab}^{i}{\\dot {\\gamma }}^{a}{\\dot {\\gamma }}^{b}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f75376ff79618b7caa18463852bfa32a6836a107)
However, as  F a b i {\displaystyle F_{ab}^{i}} ![{\\displaystyle F_{ab}^{i}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/fe73065cedbcc1ff253979009cce75b12ce162b3) is anti-symmetric in the indices  a {\displaystyle a} ![{\\displaystyle a}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ffd2487510aa438433a2579450ab2b3d557e5edc) and  b {\displaystyle b} ![{\\displaystyle b}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f11423fbb2e967f986e36804a8ae4271734917c3) this vanishes (this assumes that  γ {\displaystyle \gamma } ![{\\displaystyle \\gamma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a223c880b0ce3da8f64ee33c4f0010beee400b1a) is not discontinuous anywhere and so the tangent vector is unique). 
With regard to loop representation, the wavefunctions  Ψ [ γ ] {\displaystyle \Psi [\gamma ]} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \[\\gamma \]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6f8d7cdd9ae00dddc142ba1e03d35be57173be57) vanish when the loop has discontinuities and are knot invariants. Such functions solve the Gauss law, the spatial diffeomorphism constraint and (formally) the Hamiltonian constraint. This yields an infinite set of exact (if only formal) solutions to all the equations of quantum general relativity![[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelliSmolin19881155%E2%80%931958-13) This generated a lot of interest in the approach and eventually led to LQG. 
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=9 "Edit section: Geometric operators, the need for intersecting Wilson loops and spin network states")]
The easiest geometric quantity is the area. Let us choose coordinates so that the surface  Σ {\displaystyle \Sigma } ![{\\displaystyle \\Sigma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9e1f558f53cda207614abdf90162266c70bc5c1e) is characterized by  x 3 = 0 {\displaystyle x^{3}=0} ![{\\displaystyle x^{3}=0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3400850cf7f705575f2ae2431e3b37449ed0315c). The area of small parallelogram of the surface  Σ {\displaystyle \Sigma } ![{\\displaystyle \\Sigma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9e1f558f53cda207614abdf90162266c70bc5c1e) is the product of length of each side times  sin ⁡ θ {\displaystyle \sin \theta } ![{\\displaystyle \\sin \\theta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/efa733f6703578b0c3af870a3170b4ab0dd99c00) where  θ {\displaystyle \theta } ![{\\displaystyle \\theta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e5ab2664b422d53eb0c7df3b87e1360d75ad9af) is the angle between the sides. Say one edge is given by the vector  u → {\displaystyle {\vec {u}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\vec {u}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/89c41e9cf70c5e5b56e2128a136985a75f90ba43) and the other by  v → {\displaystyle {\vec {v}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\vec {v}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/85820588abd7333ef4d0c56539cb31c20e730753) then,  A = ‖ u → ‖ ‖ v → ‖ sin ⁡ θ = ‖ u → ‖ 2 ‖ v → ‖ 2 ( 1 − cos 2 ⁡ θ ) = ‖ u → ‖ 2 ‖ v → ‖ 2 − ( u → ⋅ v → ) 2 {\displaystyle A=\|{\vec {u}}\|\|{\vec {v}}\|\sin \theta ={\sqrt {\|{\vec {u}}\|^{2}\|{\vec {v}}\|^{2}(1-\cos ^{2}\theta )}}={\sqrt {\|{\vec {u}}\|^{2}\|{\vec {v}}\|^{2}-({\vec {u}}\cdot {\vec {v}})^{2}}}} ![{\\displaystyle A=\\|{\\vec {u}}\\|\\|{\\vec {v}}\\|\\sin \\theta ={\\sqrt {\\|{\\vec {u}}\\|^{2}\\|{\\vec {v}}\\|^{2}\(1-\\cos ^{2}\\theta \)}}={\\sqrt {\\|{\\vec {u}}\\|^{2}\\|{\\vec {v}}\\|^{2}-\({\\vec {u}}\\cdot {\\vec {v}}\)^{2}}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/71dd0db1384ec7631c4dd7ff34e389aa64ca254c)
In the space spanned by  x 1 {\displaystyle x^{1}} ![{\\displaystyle x^{1}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/92004fb9ae631cb2d99594f120855e50b75578e3) and  x 2 {\displaystyle x^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle x^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cf0bf28fd28f45d07e1ceb909ce333c18c558c93) there is an infinitesimal parallelogram described by  u → = e → 1 d x 1 {\displaystyle {\vec {u}}={\vec {e}}_{1}dx^{1}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\vec {u}}={\\vec {e}}_{1}dx^{1}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3427bd2de3198723316ea52b85ada26195efbb2e) and  v → = e → 2 d x 2 {\displaystyle {\vec {v}}={\vec {e}}_{2}dx^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\vec {v}}={\\vec {e}}_{2}dx^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6f00618a61b54cb6aa313617066b2ab00dc4ba18). Using  q A B ( 2 ) = e → A ⋅ e → B {\displaystyle q_{AB}^{(2)}={\vec {e}}_{A}\cdot {\vec {e}}_{B}} ![{\\displaystyle q_{AB}^{\(2\)}={\\vec {e}}_{A}\\cdot {\\vec {e}}_{B}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8e6719e96d7b489b76fdf4d1c13ea9216542e9b3) (where the indices  A {\displaystyle A} ![{\\displaystyle A}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7daff47fa58cdfd29dc333def748ff5fa4c923e3) and  B {\displaystyle B} ![{\\displaystyle B}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/47136aad860d145f75f3eed3022df827cee94d7a) run from 1 to 2), yields the area of the surface  Σ {\displaystyle \Sigma } ![{\\displaystyle \\Sigma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9e1f558f53cda207614abdf90162266c70bc5c1e) given by  A Σ = ∫ Σ d x 1 d x 2 det ( q ( 2 ) ) {\displaystyle A_{\Sigma }=\int _{\Sigma }dx^{1}dx^{2}{\sqrt {\det \left(q^{(2)}\right)}}} ![{\\displaystyle A_{\\Sigma }=\\int _{\\Sigma }dx^{1}dx^{2}{\\sqrt {\\det \\left\(q^{\(2\)}\\right\)}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3e2c646938ce65d59d2cecef2418f3549bf486eb) where  det ( q ( 2 ) ) = q 11 q 22 − q 12 2 {\displaystyle \det(q^{(2)})=q_{11}q_{22}-q_{12}^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle \\det\(q^{\(2\)}\)=q_{11}q_{22}-q_{12}^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/27ebd1c6174d17e239667183eca695eb1a7630a0) and is the determinant of the metric induced on  Σ {\displaystyle \Sigma } ![{\\displaystyle \\Sigma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9e1f558f53cda207614abdf90162266c70bc5c1e). The latter can be rewritten  det ( q ( 2 ) ) = ϵ A B ϵ C D q A C q B D / 2 {\displaystyle \det(q^{(2)})=\epsilon ^{AB}\epsilon ^{CD}q_{AC}q_{BD}/2} ![{\\displaystyle \\det\(q^{\(2\)}\)=\\epsilon ^{AB}\\epsilon ^{CD}q_{AC}q_{BD}/2}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7dd8cdd96356fd96abca867b3c7b972d1cec1011) where the indices  A … D {\displaystyle A\dots D} ![{\\displaystyle A\\dots D}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a354839dde05ddaac20d26c06d82c61eb1da67fe) go from 1 to 2. This can be further rewritten as  det ( q ( 2 ) ) = ϵ 3 a b ϵ 3 c d q a c q b c 2 . {\displaystyle \det(q^{(2)})={\epsilon ^{3ab}\epsilon ^{3cd}q_{ac}q_{bc} \over 2}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\det\(q^{\(2\)}\)={\\epsilon ^{3ab}\\epsilon ^{3cd}q_{ac}q_{bc} \\over 2}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b7d4a29df2f80ffc3bd7e3728f02d6e2f87ad84c)
The standard formula for an inverse matrix is  q a b = ϵ b c d ϵ a e f q c e q d f 2 ! det ( q ) . {\displaystyle q^{ab}={\epsilon ^{bcd}\epsilon ^{aef}q_{ce}q_{df} \over 2!\det(q)}.} ![{\\displaystyle q^{ab}={\\epsilon ^{bcd}\\epsilon ^{aef}q_{ce}q_{df} \\over 2!\\det\(q\)}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/053854fb6f466f0d3ac272fe430f2ed1066aa762)
There is a similarity between this and the expression for  det ( q ( 2 ) ) {\displaystyle \det(q^{(2)})} ![{\\displaystyle \\det\(q^{\(2\)}\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4c7adce34a3e1f860c2d83469d3786acfc3b0a88). But in Ashtekar variables,  E ~ i a E ~ b i = det ( q ) q a b {\displaystyle {\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}{\tilde {E}}^{bi}=\det(q)q^{ab}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}{\\tilde {E}}^{bi}=\\det\(q\)q^{ab}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e10e6dc6f3dc3ca031717fc397192dd9d0e0ae74). Therefore,  A Σ = ∫ Σ d x 1 d x 2 E ~ i 3 E ~ 3 i . {\displaystyle A_{\Sigma }=\int _{\Sigma }dx^{1}dx^{2}{\sqrt {{\tilde {E}}_{i}^{3}{\tilde {E}}^{3i}}}.} ![{\\displaystyle A_{\\Sigma }=\\int _{\\Sigma }dx^{1}dx^{2}{\\sqrt {{\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{3}{\\tilde {E}}^{3i}}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2b108393b261667d415cb857e53a9fd9c4579dac)
According to the rules of canonical quantization the triads  E ~ i 3 {\displaystyle {\tilde {E}}_{i}^{3}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{3}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ac8fd3173564c0047412ea443460faf0c9951c6f) should be promoted to quantum operators,  E ~ ^ i 3 ∼ δ δ A 3 i . {\displaystyle {\hat {\tilde {E}}}_{i}^{3}\sim {\delta \over \delta A_{3}^{i}}.} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {\\tilde {E}}}_{i}^{3}\\sim {\\delta  \\over \\delta A_{3}^{i}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/5fd40a4c6c6b06a97e6aaf81e9662dcd960baf00)
The area  A Σ {\displaystyle A_{\Sigma }} ![{\\displaystyle A_{\\Sigma }}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2d50de0a37a00b865e850b0000afaa307635b73a) can be promoted to a well defined quantum operator despite the fact that it contains a product of two functional derivatives and a square-root.[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGambiniPullin2011Section_8.2-14) Putting  N = 2 J {\displaystyle N=2J} ![{\\displaystyle N=2J}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/da29fb889ca26e0d0ff4af69465a9c43e271e1cd) ( J {\displaystyle J} ![{\\displaystyle J}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/359e4f407b49910e02c27c2f52e87a36cd74c053)-th representation),  ∑ i T i T i = J ( J + 1 ) 1. {\displaystyle \sum _{i}T^{i}T^{i}=J(J+1)1.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sum _{i}T^{i}T^{i}=J\(J+1\)1.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/00f2f399574aff422dad6dd0762cb4c76e85090f)
This quantity is important in the final formula for the area spectrum. The result is  A ^ Σ W γ [ A ] = 8 π ℓ Planck 2 β ∑ I j I ( j I + 1 ) W γ [ A ] {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}_{\Sigma }W_{\gamma }[A]=8\pi \ell _{\text{Planck}}^{2}\beta \sum _{I}{\sqrt {j_{I}(j_{I}+1)}}W_{\gamma }[A]} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}_{\\Sigma }W_{\\gamma }\[A\]=8\\pi \\ell _{\\text{Planck}}^{2}\\beta \\sum _{I}{\\sqrt {j_{I}\(j_{I}+1\)}}W_{\\gamma }\[A\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/528c35317eb5a6b86a3ce3b2b2d04bb72aa5223d) where the sum is over all edges  I {\displaystyle I} ![{\\displaystyle I}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/535ea7fc4134a31cbe2251d9d3511374bc41be9f) of the Wilson loop that pierce the surface  Σ {\displaystyle \Sigma } ![{\\displaystyle \\Sigma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9e1f558f53cda207614abdf90162266c70bc5c1e). 
The formula for the volume of a region  R {\displaystyle R} ![{\\displaystyle R}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4b0bfb3769bf24d80e15374dc37b0441e2616e33) is given by  V = ∫ R d 3 x det ( q ) = ∫ R d x 3 1 3 ! ϵ a b c ϵ i j k E ~ i a E ~ j b E ~ k c . {\displaystyle V=\int _{R}d^{3}x{\sqrt {\det(q)}}=\int _{R}dx^{3}{\sqrt {{\frac {1}{3!}}\epsilon _{abc}\epsilon ^{ijk}{\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}{\tilde {E}}_{j}^{b}{\tilde {E}}_{k}^{c}}}.} ![{\\displaystyle V=\\int _{R}d^{3}x{\\sqrt {\\det\(q\)}}=\\int _{R}dx^{3}{\\sqrt {{\\frac {1}{3!}}\\epsilon _{abc}\\epsilon ^{ijk}{\\tilde {E}}_{i}^{a}{\\tilde {E}}_{j}^{b}{\\tilde {E}}_{k}^{c}}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/49cbcee89fb545a23242d2764d339e02f100ab91)
The quantization of the volume proceeds the same way as with the area. Each time the derivative is taken, it brings down the tangent vector  γ ˙ a {\displaystyle {\dot {\gamma }}^{a}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\dot {\\gamma }}^{a}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ab63ff919cd5021367eefd0d38b54b054b8dabdb), and when the volume operator acts on non-intersecting Wilson loops the result vanishes. Quantum states with non-zero volume must therefore involve intersections. Given that the anti-symmetric summation is taken over in the formula for the volume, it needs intersections with at least three non-[coplanar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coplanar "Coplanar") lines. At least four-valent vertices are needed for the volume operator to be non-vanishing. 
Assuming the real representation where the gauge group is  SU ⁡ ( 2 ) {\displaystyle \operatorname {SU} (2)} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {SU} \(2\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/411cfb70f758af1b407762245b1b07f6ee96c526), Wilson loops are an over complete basis as there are identities relating different Wilson loops. These occur because Wilson loops are based on matrices (the holonomy) and these matrices satisfy identities. Given any two  SU ⁡ ( 2 ) {\displaystyle \operatorname {SU} (2)} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {SU} \(2\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/411cfb70f758af1b407762245b1b07f6ee96c526) matrices  A {\displaystyle \mathbb {A} } ![{\\displaystyle \\mathbb {A} }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3fb423c16a5f403edbaf66438b75e7a36e725af6) and  B {\displaystyle \mathbb {B} } ![{\\displaystyle \\mathbb {B} }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/12f0c20e9335038ffebc3536dc301978226675a3),  Tr ⁡ ( A ) Tr ⁡ ( B ) = Tr ⁡ ( A B ) + Tr ⁡ ( A B − 1 ) . {\displaystyle \operatorname {Tr} (\mathbb {A} )\operatorname {Tr} (\mathbb {B} )=\operatorname {Tr} (\mathbb {A} \mathbb {B} )+\operatorname {Tr} (\mathbb {A} \mathbb {B} ^{-1}).} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {Tr} \(\\mathbb {A} \)\\operatorname {Tr} \(\\mathbb {B} \)=\\operatorname {Tr} \(\\mathbb {A} \\mathbb {B} \)+\\operatorname {Tr} \(\\mathbb {A} \\mathbb {B} ^{-1}\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ddc338798f01fe8bd5796229f85ab544a2f60c83)
This implies that given two loops  γ {\displaystyle \gamma } ![{\\displaystyle \\gamma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a223c880b0ce3da8f64ee33c4f0010beee400b1a) and  η {\displaystyle \eta } ![{\\displaystyle \\eta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e4d701857cf5fbec133eebaf94deadf722537f64) that intersect,  W γ [ A ] W η [ A ] = W γ ∘ η [ A ] + W γ ∘ η − 1 [ A ] {\displaystyle W_{\gamma }[A]W_{\eta }[A]=W_{\gamma \circ \eta }[A]+W_{\gamma \circ \eta ^{-1}}[A]} ![{\\displaystyle W_{\\gamma }\[A\]W_{\\eta }\[A\]=W_{\\gamma \\circ \\eta }\[A\]+W_{\\gamma \\circ \\eta ^{-1}}\[A\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/aec1b1ef8986563650edd01ae423d6d5ed3d9d02) where by  η − 1 {\displaystyle \eta ^{-1}} ![{\\displaystyle \\eta ^{-1}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3764b1d639ecabf580f21bb6b1d50aec4f308225) we mean the loop  η {\displaystyle \eta } ![{\\displaystyle \\eta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e4d701857cf5fbec133eebaf94deadf722537f64) traversed in the opposite direction and  γ ∘ η {\displaystyle \gamma \circ \eta } ![{\\displaystyle \\gamma \\circ \\eta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/1106c533a3a9e59f85c98d63ba07caf3991b5beb) means the loop obtained by going around the loop  γ {\displaystyle \gamma } ![{\\displaystyle \\gamma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a223c880b0ce3da8f64ee33c4f0010beee400b1a) and then along  η {\displaystyle \eta } ![{\\displaystyle \\eta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e4d701857cf5fbec133eebaf94deadf722537f64). See figure below. Given that the matrices are unitary one has that  W γ [ A ] = W γ − 1 [ A ] {\displaystyle W_{\gamma }[A]=W_{\gamma ^{-1}}[A]} ![{\\displaystyle W_{\\gamma }\[A\]=W_{\\gamma ^{-1}}\[A\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/161f1ab6873fcd4debf2cb20230778880296970f). Also given the cyclic property of the matrix traces (i.e.  Tr ⁡ ( A B ) = Tr ⁡ ( B A ) {\displaystyle \operatorname {Tr} (\mathbb {A} \mathbb {B} )=\operatorname {Tr} (\mathbb {B} \mathbb {A} )} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {Tr} \(\\mathbb {A} \\mathbb {B} \)=\\operatorname {Tr} \(\\mathbb {B} \\mathbb {A} \)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a9e901b987119b626bddfb4d4171fa7ff7dc0185)) one has that  W γ ∘ η [ A ] = W η ∘ γ [ A ] {\displaystyle W_{\gamma \circ \eta }[A]=W_{\eta \circ \gamma }[A]} ![{\\displaystyle W_{\\gamma \\circ \\eta }\[A\]=W_{\\eta \\circ \\gamma }\[A\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/11f9f68574bd299d5d4400bf7239b6b295942567). These identities can be combined with each other into further identities of increasing complexity adding more loops. These identities are the so-called Mandelstam identities. Spin networks certain are linear combinations of intersecting Wilson loops designed to address the over-completeness introduced by the Mandelstam identities (for trivalent intersections they eliminate the over-completeness entirely) and actually constitute a basis for all gauge invariant functions. 
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/5/5f/The_Mandelstam_identity.jpg/759px-The_Mandelstam_identity.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_Mandelstam_identity.jpg)Graphical representation of the simplest non-trivial Mandelstam identity relating different [Wilson loops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilson_loops "Wilson loops")
As mentioned above the holonomy tells one how to propagate test spin half particles. A spin network state assigns an amplitude to a set of spin half particles tracing out a path in space, merging and splitting. These are described by spin networks  γ {\displaystyle \gamma } ![{\\displaystyle \\gamma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a223c880b0ce3da8f64ee33c4f0010beee400b1a): the edges are labelled by spins together with 'intertwiners' at the vertices which are prescription for how to sum over different ways the spins are rerouted. The sum over rerouting are chosen as such to make the form of the intertwiner invariant under Gauss gauge transformations. 
### Hamiltonian constraint of LQG
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=10 "Edit section: Hamiltonian constraint of LQG")]
Main article: [Hamiltonian constraint of LQG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamiltonian_constraint_of_LQG "Hamiltonian constraint of LQG")
In the long history of canonical quantum gravity formulating the Hamiltonian constraint as a quantum operator ([Wheeler–DeWitt equation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheeler%E2%80%93DeWitt_equation "Wheeler–DeWitt equation")) in a mathematically rigorous manner has been a formidable problem. It was in the loop representation that a mathematically well defined Hamiltonian constraint was finally formulated in 1996.[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThiemann1996257%E2%80%93264-10) We leave more details of its construction to the article [Hamiltonian constraint of LQG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamiltonian_constraint_of_LQG "Hamiltonian constraint of LQG"). This together with the quantum versions of the Gauss law and spatial diffeomorphism constrains written in the loop representation are the central equations of LQG (modern canonical quantum General relativity). 
Finding the states that are annihilated by these constraints (the physical states), and finding the corresponding physical inner product, and observables is the main goal of the technical side of LQG. 
An important aspect of the Hamiltonian operator is that it only acts at vertices (a consequence of this is that Thiemann's Hamiltonian operator, like Ashtekar's operator, annihilates non-intersecting loops except now it is not just formal and has rigorous mathematical meaning). More precisely, its action is non-zero on at least vertices of valence three and greater and results in a linear combination of new spin networks where the original graph has been modified by the addition of lines at each vertex together and a change in the labels of the adjacent links of the vertex.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]
### Chiral fermions and the fermion doubling problem
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=11 "Edit section: Chiral fermions and the fermion doubling problem")]
A significant challenge in theoretical physics lies in unifying LQG, a theory of quantum spacetime, with the [Standard Model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Model "Standard Model") of particle physics, which describes fundamental forces and particles. A major obstacle in this endeavor is the [fermion doubling problem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fermion_doubling_problem "Fermion doubling problem"), which arises when incorporating [chiral fermions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiral_fermion "Chiral fermion") into the LQG framework. 
Chiral fermions, such as electrons and quarks, are fundamental particles characterized by their "handedness" or chirality. This property dictates that a particle and its mirror image behave differently under weak interactions. This asymmetry is fundamental to the Standard Model's success in explaining numerous physical phenomena. 
However, attempts to integrate chiral fermions into LQG often result in the appearance of spurious, mirror-image particles. Instead of a single left-handed fermion, for instance, the theory predicts the existence of both a left-handed and a right-handed version.[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-15) This "doubling" contradicts the observed chirality of the Standard Model and disrupts its predictive power. 
The fermion doubling problem poses a significant hurdle in constructing a consistent theory of quantum gravity. The Standard Model's accuracy in describing the universe at the smallest scales relies heavily on the unique properties of chiral fermions. Without a solution to this problem, incorporating matter and its interactions into a unified framework of quantum gravity remains a significant challenge. 
Therefore, resolving the fermion doubling problem is crucial for advancing our understanding of the universe at its most fundamental level and developing a complete theory that unites gravity with the quantum world. 
## Spin foams
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=12 "Edit section: Spin foams")]
Main articles: [spin network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spin_network "Spin network"), [spin foam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spin_foam "Spin foam"), [BF model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BF_model "BF model"), and [Barrett–Crane model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrett%E2%80%93Crane_model "Barrett–Crane model")
In loop quantum gravity (LQG), a spin network represents a "quantum state" of the gravitational field on a 3-dimensional [hypersurface](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypersurface "Hypersurface"). The set of all possible spin networks (or, more accurately, "s-knots" – that is, equivalence classes of spin networks under diffeomorphisms) is countable; it constitutes a basis of LQG [Hilbert space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilbert_space "Hilbert space"). 
In physics, a spin foam is a topological structure made out of two-dimensional faces that represents one of the configurations that must be summed to obtain a Feynman's path integral (functional integration) description of quantum gravity. It is closely related to loop quantum gravity. 
### Spin foam derived from the Hamiltonian constraint operator
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=13 "Edit section: Spin foam derived from the Hamiltonian constraint operator")]
The Hamiltonian constraint generates 'time' evolution. Solving the Hamiltonian constraint should tell us how quantum states evolve in 'time' from an initial spin network state to a final spin network state. One approach to solving the Hamiltonian constraint starts with what is called the [Dirac delta function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirac_delta_function "Dirac delta function"). The summation of which over different sequences of actions can be visualized as a summation over different histories of 'interaction vertices' in the 'time' evolution sending the initial spin network to the final spin network. Each time a Hamiltonian operator acts it does so by adding a new edge at the vertex.[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThiemann2008458%E2%80%93462-16)
This then naturally gives rise to the two-complex (a combinatorial set of faces that join along edges, which in turn join on vertices) underlying the spin foam description; we evolve forward an initial spin network sweeping out a surface, the action of the Hamiltonian constraint operator is to produce a new planar surface starting at the vertex. We are able to use the action of the Hamiltonian constraint on the vertex of a spin network state to associate an amplitude to each "interaction" (in analogy to [Feynman diagrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feynman_diagrams "Feynman diagrams")). See figure below. This opens a way of trying to directly link canonical LQG to a path integral description. Just as a spin networks describe quantum space, each configuration contributing to these path integrals, or sums over history, describe 'quantum spacetime'. Because of their resemblance to soap foams and the way they are labeled [John Baez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Baez "John Baez") gave these 'quantum spacetimes' the name 'spin foams'. 
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/3/34/Spin_foam_from_Hamiltonian_constraint.jpg/960px-Spin_foam_from_Hamiltonian_constraint.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Spin_foam_from_Hamiltonian_constraint.jpg)The action of the Hamiltonian constraint translated to the [path integral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functional_integration "Functional integration") or so-called spin foam description. A single node splits into three nodes, creating a spin foam vertex.  N ( x n ) {\displaystyle N(x_{n})} ![{\\displaystyle N\(x_{n}\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6407f8e2fbf9066a5e1f35a899681330120dfe8c) is the value of  N {\displaystyle N} ![{\\displaystyle N}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f5e3890c981ae85503089652feb48b191b57aae3) at the vertex and  H n o p {\displaystyle H_{nop}} ![{\\displaystyle H_{nop}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/611d6fe0a85ef03dcfa5a0867068d79d35a69475) are the matrix elements of the Hamiltonian constraint  H ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {H}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {H}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6bb06de5217295d7fbdbf68fb9c5309a513fc99e).
There are however severe difficulties with this particular approach, for example the Hamiltonian operator is not self-adjoint, in fact it is not even a [normal operator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normal_operator "Normal operator") (i.e. the operator does not commute with its adjoint) and so the [spectral theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectral_theorem "Spectral theorem") cannot be used to define the exponential in general. The most serious problem is that the  H ^ ( x ) {\displaystyle {\hat {H}}(x)} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {H}}\(x\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/86863842d5c484075e8f944f7a4ac6661f402c81)'s are not mutually commuting, it can then be shown the formal quantity  ∫ [ d N ] e i ∫ d 3 x N ( x ) H ^ ( x ) {\textstyle \int [dN]e^{i\int d^{3}xN(x){\hat {H}}(x)}} ![{\\textstyle \\int \[dN\]e^{i\\int d^{3}xN\(x\){\\hat {H}}\(x\)}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/db38100e52a146572ef04876194c32d69699e434) cannot even define a (generalized) projector. The master constraint (see below) does not suffer from these problems and as such offers a way of connecting the canonical theory to the path integral formulation. 
### Spin foams from BF theory
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Loop_quantum_gravity&action=edit&section=14 "Edit section: Spin foams from BF theory")]
It turns out there are alternative routes to formulating the path integral, however their connection to the Hamiltonian formalism is less clear. One way is to start with the [BF theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BF_theory "BF theory"). This is a simpler theory than general relativity, it has no local degrees of freedom and as such depends only on topological aspects of the fields. BF theory is what is known as a [topological field theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topological_field_theory "Topological field theory"). Surprisingly, it turns out that general relativity can be obtained from BF theory by imposing a constraint,[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBojowaldPerez2009877-17) BF theory involves a field  B a b I J {\displaystyle B_{ab}^{IJ}} ![{\\displaystyle B_{ab}^{IJ}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/828b649d407891bacb8c8d35c73c43fcfd7ea028) and if one chooses the field  B {\displaystyle B} ![{\\displaystyle B}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/47136aad860d145f75f3eed3022df827cee94d7a) to be the (anti-symmetric) product of two tetrads  B a b I J = 1 2 ( E a I E b J − E b I E a J ) {\displaystyle B_{ab}^{IJ}={1 \over 2}\left(E_{a}^{I}E_{b}^{J}-E_{b}^{I}E_{a}^{J}\right)} ![{\\displaystyle B_{ab}^{IJ}={1 \\over 2}\\left\(E_{a}^{I}E_{b}^{J}-E_{b}^{I}E_{a}^{J}\\right\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/44b102cc85af4ff01730539058a4c817a9cc9497) (tetrads are like triads but in four spacetime dimensions), one recovers general relativity. The condition that the  B {\displaystyle B} ![{\\displaystyle B}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/47136aad860d145f75f3eed3022df827cee94d7a) field be given by the product of two tetrads is called the simplicity constraint. The spin foam dynamics of the topological field theory is well understood. Given the spin foam 'interaction' amplitudes for this simple theory, one then tries to implement the simplicity conditions to obtain a path integral for general relativity. The non-trivial task of constructing a spin foam model is then reduced to the question of how this simplicity constraint should be imposed in the quantum theory. The first attempt at this was the famous [Barrett–Crane model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrett%E2%80%93Crane_model "Barrett–Crane model").[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrettCrane20003101%E2%80%933118-18) However this model was shown to be problematic, for example there did not seem to be enough degrees of freedom to ensure the correct classical limit.[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_quantum_gravity#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelliAlesci2007104012-19) It has been argued that the simplicity constraint was imposed too strongly at the quantum level and should only be imposed in the sense of expectation values just as with the [Lorenz gauge condition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorenz_gauge_condition "Lorenz gauge condition") ∂ μ A ^ μ {\displaystyle \partial _{\mu }{\hat {A}}^{\mu }} ![{\\displaystyle \\partial _{\\mu }{\\hat {A}}^{\\mu }}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/45f47e7eff9089848ab39d5aa543040a7eb4ae51) in the [Gupta–Bleuler formalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta%E2%80%93Bleuler_formalism "Gupta–Bleuler formalism") of [quantum electrodynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_electrodynamics "Quantum electrodynamics"). New models have now been put forward, sometimes motivated by imposing the simplicity conditions in a weaker sense.
